----- step5_report.txt -----
# Step 5 report: split-conformal false alarm control

clean frames: q1 1222 (first half 610, second half 612), q2 1225; propagated faults 50

  FAR = false alarm rate on clean q2 frames, 95% moving-block bootstrap interval (blocks of 60 frames)
  TPR = detected fraction of propagated transient faults (Wilson interval)
  split-q12 uses q2 to set the threshold, so its q2 FAR is in-sample by construction
  a conformal threshold needs at least 1/alpha - 1 calibration frames; with fewer it is infinite (no alarms)

  what to compare: FAR and worst seq should move toward alpha as gamma grows (gamma 0 is the step 5
  baseline), and TPR shows what the adaptation costs in detection power. Guarantee per stream of T frames:
  |realized rate - alpha| <= (max(alpha, 1 - alpha) + gamma) / (gamma * T).


----- step7_report.txt -----
# Step 7 report: weight checksum

per-layer checksum (64 small reductions): 4.017 ms, 200/200 flips caught
weights moved into one buffer: outputs unchanged True, inference 12.02 ms per frame afterwards
conv/linear weights only: 11,146,096 values, 0.496 ms; all parameters and buffers: 11,156,544 values (99.9% were covered before), 200/200 random flips anywhere caught, outputs unchanged True
timing (yolo): integer checksum of all parameters 0.496 ms, inference 13.23 ms per frame; 200/200 random single bit flips changed the checksum

corrupting weight faults replayed: 106 (of 1500), window 25 frames;
without any check they corrupt detector output on 22.7 frames on average and all of them change tracking

  check every     overhead   still change tracking   corrupted frames
  1 frames           3.75%                    0.0%               0.00
  2 frames           1.87%                   27.4%               0.49
  5 frames           0.75%                   49.1%               1.94
  10 frames          0.37%                   60.2%               4.33
  25 frames          0.15%                   77.2%              11.20

  reading: the checksum has no false alarms and catches every weight fault, harmful or not; the only
  question is how often to run it. Black-box monitors stay responsible for transient (activation) faults,
  which a weight checksum cannot see. Together they cover both fault types at a known cost.

# Step 5 report: split-conformal false alarm control

clean frames: q1 1679 (first half 833, second half 846), q2 1690; propagated faults 20

  FAR = false alarm rate on clean q2 frames, 95% moving-block bootstrap interval (blocks of 60 frames)
  TPR = detected fraction of propagated transient faults (Wilson interval)
  split-q12 uses q2 to set the threshold, so its q2 FAR is in-sample by construction
  a conformal threshold needs at least 1/alpha - 1 calibration frames; with fewer it is infinite (no alarms)

  what to compare: FAR and worst seq should move toward alpha as gamma grows (gamma 0 is the step 5
  baseline), and TPR shows what the adaptation costs in detection power. Guarantee per stream of T frames:
  |realized rate - alpha| <= (max(alpha, 1 - alpha) + gamma) / (gamma * T).

weights moved into one buffer: outputs unchanged True, inference 11.47 ms per frame afterwards
conv/linear weights only: 11,146,096 values, 0.496 ms; all parameters and buffers: 11,156,544 values (99.9% were covered before), 200/200 random flips anywhere caught, outputs unchanged True
timing (yolo): integer checksum of all parameters 0.492 ms, inference 11.16 ms per frame; 200/200 random single bit flips changed the checksum

corrupting weight faults replayed: 64 (of 1500), window 25 frames;
without any check they corrupt detector output on 19.4 frames on average and all of them change tracking

  check every     overhead   still change tracking   corrupted frames
  1 frames           4.41%                    0.0%               0.00
  2 frames           2.21%                   28.1%               0.41
  5 frames           0.88%                   51.9%               1.67
  10 frames          0.44%                   66.7%               3.72
  25 frames          0.18%                   82.6%               9.59

  reading: the checksum has no false alarms and catches every weight fault, harmful or not; the only
  question is how often to run it. Black-box monitors stay responsible for transient (activation) faults,
  which a weight checksum cannot see. Together they cover both fault types at a known cost.

  baseline), and TPR shows what the adaptation costs in detection power. Guarantee per stream of T frames:
  |realized rate - alpha| <= (max(alpha, 1 - alpha) + gamma) / (gamma * T).

timing (yolo): integer checksum of all parameters 1.395 ms, inference 29.67 ms per frame; 200/200 random single bit flips changed the checksum

corrupting weight faults replayed: 203 (of 1500), window 25 frames;
without any check they corrupt detector output on 23.7 frames on average and all of them change tracking

  check every     overhead   still change tracking   corrupted frames
  1 frames           4.70%                    0.0%               0.00
  2 frames           2.35%                   11.8%               0.49
  5 frames           0.94%                   23.7%               1.94
  10 frames          0.47%                   34.1%               4.35
  25 frames          0.19%                   59.9%              11.50

  reading: the checksum has no false alarms and catches every weight fault, harmful or not; the only
  question is how often to run it. Black-box monitors stay responsible for transient (activation) faults,
  which a weight checksum cannot see. Together they cover both fault types at a known cost.

# Step 5 report: split-conformal false alarm control

clean frames: q1 1222 (first half 610, second half 612), q2 1225; propagated faults 71

  FAR = false alarm rate on clean q2 frames, 95% moving-block bootstrap interval (blocks of 60 frames)
  TPR = detected fraction of propagated transient faults (Wilson interval)
  split-q12 uses q2 to set the threshold, so its q2 FAR is in-sample by construction
  a conformal threshold needs at least 1/alpha - 1 calibration frames; with fewer it is infinite (no alarms)

timing (yolo): integer checksum of all parameters 0.420 ms, inference 17.32 ms per frame; 200/200 random single bit flips changed the checksum

corrupting weight faults replayed: 154 (of 1500), window 25 frames;
without any check they corrupt detector output on 23.3 frames on average and all of them change tracking

  check every     overhead   still change tracking   corrupted frames
  1 frames           2.43%                    0.0%               0.00
  2 frames           1.21%                   21.8%               0.49
  5 frames           0.49%                   40.4%               1.96
  10 frames          0.24%                   52.0%               4.38
  25 frames          0.10%                   72.4%              11.44

  reading: the checksum has no false alarms and catches every weight fault, harmful or not; the only
  question is how often to run it. Black-box monitors stay responsible for transient (activation) faults,
  which a weight checksum cannot see. Together they cover both fault types at a known cost.

clean frames: q1 1222 (first half 610, second half 612), q2 1225; propagated faults 85

  FAR = false alarm rate on clean q2 frames, 95% moving-block bootstrap interval (blocks of 60 frames)
  TPR = detected fraction of propagated transient faults (Wilson interval)
  split-q12 uses q2 to set the threshold, so its q2 FAR is in-sample by construction

timing (yolo): integer checksum of all parameters 1.128 ms, inference 14.66 ms per frame; 200/200 random single bit flips changed the checksum

corrupting weight faults replayed: 104 (of 1500), window 25 frames;
without any check they corrupt detector output on 22.5 frames on average and all of them change tracking

  check every     overhead   still change tracking   corrupted frames
  1 frames           7.70%                    0.0%               0.00
  2 frames           3.85%                   27.4%               0.49
  5 frames           1.54%                   47.7%               1.96
  10 frames          0.77%                   58.5%               4.36
  25 frames          0.31%                   76.2%              11.21

  reading: the checksum has no false alarms and catches every weight fault, harmful or not; the only
  question is how often to run it. Black-box monitors stay responsible for transient (activation) faults,
  which a weight checksum cannot see. Together they cover both fault types at a known cost.

# Step 5 report: split-conformal false alarm control

clean frames: q1 1222 (first half 610, second half 612), q2 1225; propagated faults 50

  FAR = false alarm rate on clean q2 frames, 95% moving-block bootstrap interval (blocks of 60 frames)
  TPR = detected fraction of propagated transient faults (Wilson interval)
  split-q12 uses q2 to set the threshold, so its q2 FAR is in-sample by construction
  a conformal threshold needs at least 1/alpha - 1 calibration frames; with fewer it is infinite (no alarms)

corrupting weight faults replayed: 117 (of 1500), window 25 frames;
without any check they corrupt detector output on 23.0 frames on average and all of them change tracking

  check every     overhead   still change tracking   corrupted frames
  1 frames           1.16%                    0.0%               0.00
  2 frames           0.58%                   23.5%               0.48
  5 frames           0.23%                   44.1%               1.94
  10 frames          0.12%                   54.4%               4.33
  25 frames          0.05%                   73.1%              11.28

  reading: the checksum has no false alarms and catches every weight fault, harmful or not; the only
  question is how often to run it. Black-box monitors stay responsible for transient (activation) faults,
  which a weight checksum cannot see. Together they cover both fault types at a known cost.

# Step 5 report: split-conformal false alarm control

clean frames: q1 1222 (first half 610, second half 612), q2 1225; propagated faults 20

  FAR = false alarm rate on clean q2 frames, 95% moving-block bootstrap interval (blocks of 60 frames)
  TPR = detected fraction of propagated transient faults (Wilson interval)
  split-q12 uses q2 to set the threshold, so its q2 FAR is in-sample by construction
  a conformal threshold needs at least 1/alpha - 1 calibration frames; with fewer it is infinite (no alarms)

corrupting weight faults replayed: 112 (of 1500), window 25 frames;
without any check they corrupt detector output on 22.4 frames on average and all of them change tracking

  check every     overhead   still change tracking   corrupted frames
  1 frames           3.84%                    0.0%               0.00
  2 frames           1.92%                   26.8%               0.48
  5 frames           0.77%                   49.6%               1.93
  10 frames          0.38%                   62.7%               4.31
  25 frames          0.15%                   80.4%              11.12

  reading: the checksum has no false alarms and catches every weight fault, harmful or not; the only
  question is how often to run it. Black-box monitors stay responsible for transient (activation) faults,
  which a weight checksum cannot see. Together they cover both fault types at a known cost.

# Step 5 report: split-conformal false alarm control

clean frames: q1 2171 (first half 1085, second half 1086), q2 2173; propagated faults 106

  FAR = false alarm rate on clean q2 frames, 95% moving-block bootstrap interval (blocks of 60 frames)
  TPR = detected fraction of propagated transient faults (Wilson interval)
  split-q12 uses q2 to set the threshold, so its q2 FAR is in-sample by construction
  a conformal threshold needs at least 1/alpha - 1 calibration frames; with fewer it is infinite (no alarms)

per-layer checksum (64 small reductions): 3.971 ms, 200/200 flips caught
weights moved into one buffer: outputs unchanged True, inference 11.70 ms per frame afterwards
conv/linear weights only: 11,146,096 values, 0.498 ms; all parameters and buffers: 11,156,544 values (99.9% were covered before), 200/200 random flips anywhere caught, outputs unchanged True
timing (yolo): integer checksum of all parameters 0.497 ms, inference 11.82 ms per frame; 200/200 random single bit flips changed the checksum

corrupting weight faults replayed: 132 (of 1500), window 25 frames;
without any check they corrupt detector output on 24.0 frames on average and all of them change tracking

  check every     overhead   still change tracking   corrupted frames
  1 frames           4.20%                    0.0%               0.00
  2 frames           2.10%                   18.9%               0.48
  5 frames           0.84%                   36.4%               1.94
  10 frames          0.42%                   47.0%               4.36
  25 frames          0.17%                   66.5%              11.57

  reading: the checksum has no false alarms and catches every weight fault, harmful or not; the only
  question is how often to run it. Black-box monitors stay responsible for transient (activation) faults,
  which a weight checksum cannot see. Together they cover both fault types at a known cost.
